{"type":"user","message":{"role":"user","content":[{"tool_use_id":"call_98b4352cdea94f749ec749cc","type":"tool_result","content":"1\t# Embedded Database Evaluation for Scrape-State Persistence\n2\t\n3\t**Evaluation Date:** 2026-08-17  \n4\t**Purpose:** Replace JSON-file-based state persistence with a proper embedded database  \n5\t**Use Case:** Track per-source-file scrape state for incremental scraping across 500K+ source files\n6\t\n7\t---\n8\t\n9\t## Executive Summary\n10\t\n11\tAfter evaluating three embedded database options (sled, redb, and SQLite), **SQLite was chosen** as the backend for scrape-state persistence. SQLite provides the best balance of crash safety, concurrency support, mature tooling, and zero external dependencies for this use case.\n12\t\n13\t**Decision:** SQLite (rusqlite with bundled feature)  \n14\t**Status:** ✅ Implemented and default in AgentScribe  \n15\t**Migration:** Automatic one-time import from legacy JSON state file\n16\t\n17\t---\n18\t\n19\t## Use Case Requirements\n20\t\n21\tThe scrape-state persistence layer must support:\n22\t\n23\t1. **O(1) updates by file path** — Update a single source file's state without rewriting the entire corpus\n24\t2. **500K+ keys** — Scale to hundreds of thousands of tracked source files across multiple agents and projects\n25\t3. **Crash-safe writes** — Process kill, disk full, or power loss must not corrupt the entire state database\n26\t4. **Concurrent access** — Multiple scraper workers or daemon + CLI may access state simultaneously\n27\t5. **Cross-platform** — Must work on Linux, macOS, and potentially Windows\n28\t6. **Zero external dependencies** — No system package dependencies or external services\n29\t7. **Embedded** — Single-process database, no separate server process\n30\t\n31\t---\n32\t\n33\t## Comparison Matrix\n34\t\n35\t| Criterion | sled | redb | SQLite (chosen) |\n36\t|-----------|------|------|------------------|\n37\t| **Type** | Pure Rust KV store | Pure Rust B+Tree KV | Relational (embedded) |\n38\t| **Performance (Read)** | Excellent (memory-mapped) | Excellent (memory-mapped) | Good (B-tree index) |\n39\t| **Performance (Write)** | Good | Good | Excellent (WAL mode) |\n40\t| **Concurrency Model** | Lock-free readers + serialized writer | Lock-free readers + serialized writer | WAL mode (multiple readers, serialized writers) |\n41\t| **Memory Footprint** | Moderate (page cache) | Low (copy-on-write tree) | Configurable (default 2MB cache) |\n42\t| **Compaction Behavior** | Background async compaction | Manual compaction required | Automatic via VACUUM or auto-vacuum |\n43\t| **Crash Safety** | Good (copy-on-write) | Good (atomic commits) | Excellent (WAL + rollback) |\n44\t| **Rust Integration** | Native (pure Rust) | Native (pure Rust) | FFI (rusqlite wrapper) |\n45\t| **Maturity** | Stable, actively maintained | Stable, actively maintained | Battle-tested (30+ years) |\n46\t| **Tooling** | Basic CLI | Basic CLI | Extensive (CLI, GUI, diff tools) |\n47\t| **Cross-Platform** | ✅ Yes | ✅ Yes | ✅ Yes |\n48\t| **Single-File Storage** | ✅ Yes | ✅ Yes | ✅ Yes (+WAL files) |\n49\t| **Dependencies** | None (pure Rust) | None (pure Rust) | None (bundled feature) |\n50\t| **Query Expressiveness** | Key-value only | Key-value only | SQL (rich queries, indexes) |\n51\t| **Schema Versioning** | Manual | Manual | Manual (migratable) |\n52\t| **Community Support** | Growing | Growing | Massive |\n53\t\n54\t---\n55\t\n56\t## Detailed Evaluation\n57\t\n58\t### sled\n59\t\n60\t**Strengths:**\n61\t- Pure Rust, idiomatic API\n62\t- Lock-free concurrency model (excellent for read-heavy workloads)\n63\t- Memory-mapped storage for fast reads\n64\t- Copy-on-write B-tree structure provides good crash safety\n65\t- Actively maintained by the FoundationDB community\n66\t\n67\t**Weaknesses:**\n68\t- Background compaction can be unpredictable at scale\n69\t- Less mature tooling compared to SQLite\n70\t- Key-value only (no secondary indexes without manual implementation)\n71\t- Smaller community, fewer battle-tested patterns\n72\t\n73\t**Verdict:** Excellent choice for pure Rust projects needing maximum performance, but overkill for our use case where query complexity is low and reliability matters more than raw throughput.\n74\t\n75\t### redb\n76\t\n77\t**Strengths:**\n78\t- Pure Rust, modern API\n79\t- Copy-on-write B+tree with efficient storage\n80\t- Very low memory footprint\n81\t- Type-safe key-value API\n82\t- Active development\n83\t\n84\t**Weaknesses:**\n85\t- Manual compaction required (can lead to bloat without maintenance)\n86\t- Less mature than sled and SQLite\n87\t- Key-value only (no SQL query layer)\n88\t- Smaller ecosystem\n89\t- Limited real-world deployment history at scale\n90\t\n91\t**Verdict:** A promising modern KV store, but lacks the battle-tested reliability and tooling ecosystem of SQLite. Manual compaction is a operational concern for long-running daemons.\n92\t\n93\t### SQLite (rusqlite)\n94\t\n95\t**Strengths:**\n96\t- Battle-tested reliability (30+ years of production use)\n97\t- WAL mode provides excellent concurrency (multiple readers, serialized writers)\n98\t- Excellent crash safety (rollback journal, atomic commits)\n99\t- Rich query language (SQL) with indexes for complex queries\n100\t- Extensive tooling (CLI, GUI browsers, diff tools)\n101\t- Zero dependencies with `rusqlite`'s bundled feature\n102\t- Automatic schema versioning support\n103\t- Cross-platform with identical behavior\n104\t\n105\t**Weaknesses:**\n106\t- FFI boundary (small performance overhead)\n107\t- Relational overhead for simple KV operations\n108\t- JSON serialization required for complex types (session_ids array)\n109\t- Requires manual pragma tuning for optimal performance\n110\t\n111\t**Verdict:** **Chosen** for our use case. The reliability and tooling ecosystem outweigh the small performance overhead. SQL provides future flexibility for queries like \"all files for plugin X\" without implementing secondary indexes manually.\n112\t\n113\t---\n114\t\n115\t## Why SQLite Was Chosen\n116\t\n117\t### Primary Reason: Crash Safety and Reliability\n118\t\n119\tPer ADR-1, AgentScribe experienced a catastrophic failure where a crashed daemon process left a 4MB JSON state file truncated, blocking scraping for 26 days. SQLite's WAL mode and atomic commits guarantee this cannot happen:\n120\t\n121\t- **Atomic commits:** Either all changes write or none do (no torn writes)\n122\t- **WAL journal:** Write-ahead logging allows rollback of incomplete transactions\n123\t- **Rollback on crash:** If a process crashes mid-transaction, SQLite rolls back the transaction on next open\n124\t- **Hot backups:** Can backup the database without stopping writes\n125\t\n126\t### Secondary Reasons\n127\t\n128\t1. **Tooling Ecosystem:** When debugging state issues, `sqlite3` CLI provides immediate visibility:\n129\t   ```bash\n130\t   sqlite3 ~/.agentscribe/state/scrape-state.db\n131\t   SELECT * FROM file_state WHERE plugin = 'claude-code' LIMIT 10;\n132\t   ```\n133\t\n134\t2. **Query Flexibility:** Future requirements may need complex queries:\n135\t   ```sql\n136\t   -- Find all files that haven't been scraped in 7 days\n137\t   SELECT file_path FROM file_state \n138\t   WHERE datetime(last_scraped) < datetime('now', '-7 days');\n139\t   \n140\t   -- Count files per plugin\n141\t   SELECT plugin, COUNT(*) FROM file_state GROUP BY plugin;\n142\t   ```\n143\t\n144\t3. **Zero Dependencies:** `rusqlite` with the `bundled` feature compiles SQLite into the binary, eliminating the need for `libsqlite3-dev` system packages. This matches AgentScribe's \"no external dependencies\" principle.\n145\t\n146\t4. **Proven at Scale:** SQLite powers Chrome, Firefox, Android, iOS, and countless embedded systems. It handles databases in the hundreds of GB range. At 500K keys (~50MB), it's not even breaking a sweat.\n147\t\n148\t5. **Automatic Migration:** The legacy JSON file migration is simple and proven—load JSON, transactionally insert rows, backup JSON file.\n149\t\n150\t---\n151\t\n152\t## Schema Design\n153\t\n154\t### Table: `file_state`\n155\t\n156\t```sql\n157\tCREATE TABLE file_state (\n158\t    file_path TEXT PRIMARY KEY,\n159\t    plugin TEXT NOT NULL,\n160\t    last_byte_offset INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,\n161\t    last_modified TEXT NOT NULL,\n162\t    last_scraped TEXT NOT NULL,\n163\t    session_ids TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT '[]',\n164\t    last_delimiter_offset INTEGER\n165\t);\n166\t```\n167\t\n168\t### Index\n169\t\n170\t```sql\n171\tCREATE INDEX idx_plugin ON file_state(plugin);\n172\t```\n173\t\n174\tSupports efficient queries like:\n175\t- `SELECT file_path FROM file_state WHERE plugin = 'claude-code'`\n176\t- `files_for_plugin()` implementation in `StateStore` trait\n177\t\n178\t### Schema Version Tracking\n179\t\n180\t```sql\n181\tCREATE TABLE schema_version (version INTEGER PRIMARY KEY);\n182\tINSERT INTO schema_version (version) VALUES (1);\n183\t```\n184\t\n185\tEnables future schema migrations (e.g., adding checksum fields, new columns).\n186\t\n187\t---\n188\t\n189\t## Key-Value Mapping\n190\t\n191\t### Key Format\n192\t\n193\t**Primary key:** `file_path` (full absolute path to source file)\n194\t\n195\tExamples:\n196\t- `/home/user/.claude/projects/-home-coding-myproject/abc123.jsonl`\n197\t- `/home/user/projects/myapp/.aider.chat.history.md`\n198\t\n199\tThis provides O(1) lookups by file path, the primary access pattern for scrape state.\n200\t\n201\t### Value Structure\n202\t\n203\tEach row contains:\n204\t\n205\t| Column | Type | Description |\n206\t|--------|------|-------------|\n207\t| `file_path` | TEXT | Absolute path to source file (primary key) |\n208\t| `plugin` | TEXT | Plugin name (e.g., \"claude-code\", \"aider\") |\n209\t| `last_byte_offset` | INTEGER | Last byte position read (for JSONL position-based resuming) |\n210\t| `last_modified` | TEXT | File modification time (ISO 8601, RFC 3339) |\n211\t| `last_scraped` | TEXT | Last scrape timestamp (ISO 8601, RFC 3339) |\n212\t| `session_ids` | TEXT | JSON array of session IDs extracted from this file |\n213\t| `last_delimiter_offset` | INTEGER | Last delimiter position for markdown-based session detection |\n214\t\n215\t**Why JSON for `session_ids`:** SQLite doesn't have a native array type. Storing as JSON array (`[\"session-1\", \"session-2\"]`) is simple and works well for our use case (small arrays, rarely queried). If this becomes a bottleneck, a separate `file_sessions` table with one row per session ID can be added.\n216\t\n217\t---\n218\t\n219\t## Migration Strategy\n220\t\n221\t### Design Principles\n222\t\n223\t1. **Automatic:** No user intervention required\n224\t2. **One-time:** Migration happens on first load, then never again\n225\t3. **Safe:** Legacy JSON file is backed up before migration\n226\t4. **Atomic:** Migration runs in a single transaction—all-or-nothing\n227\t5. **Detectable:** User can see migration happened in logs and by backup file extension\n228\t\n229\t### Implementation\n230\t\n231\t**Trigger:** First launch after code update, when:\n232\t- `scrape-state.db` does not exist\n233\t- `scrape-state.json` exists (legacy file)\n234\t\n235\t**Steps:**\n236\t\n237\t1. **Detect migration needed:** On `SqliteStateManager::new()`, check if legacy JSON exists\n238\t2. **Create schema:** Initialize `file_state` table and indexes\n239\t3. **Load JSON:** Read `scrape-state.json` and parse as `ScrapeState`\n240\t4. **Begin transaction:** Start SQLite transaction for atomic import\n241\t5. **Import rows:** For each `(file_path, file_state)` in JSON:\n242\t   - Serialize `session_ids` array to JSON string\n243\t   - Format timestamps as RFC 3339\n244\t   - Execute `INSERT INTO file_state ... VALUES (...)`\n245\t6. **Commit transaction:** Atomic commit of all rows\n246\t7. **Backup JSON:** Rename `scrape-state.json` → `scrape-state.json.migrated`\n247\t8. **Log success:** Info-level log with source and backup paths\n248\t\n249\t**Rollback:** If any step fails (JSON parse error, SQLite constraint violation), the transaction is rolled back and the JSON file remains untouched. Next launch retries migration.\n250\t\n251\t### Code Implementation\n252\t\n253\tSee `src/scraper/state_sqlite.rs::migrate_from_json()`:\n254\t\n255\t```rust\n256\tfn migrate_from_json(&self, conn: &mut Connection, json_path: &Path) -> Result<()> {\n257\t    tracing::info!(path = %json_path.display(), \"Migrating scrape state from JSON to SQLite\");\n258\t\n259\t    let json_content = std::fs::read_to_string(json_path)?;\n260\t    let scrape_state: ScrapeState = serde_json::from_str(&json_content)?;\n261\t\n262\t    let tx = conn.transaction()?;\n263\t\n264\t    for (file_path, file_state) in &scrape_state.sources {\n265\t        let session_ids_json = serde_json::to_string(&file_state.session_ids)?;\n266\t        let last_modified = file_state.last_modified.to_rfc3339();\n267\t        let last_scraped = file_state.last_scraped.to_rfc3339();\n268\t\n269\t        tx.execute(\n270\t            \"INSERT INTO file_state (file_path, plugin, last_byte_offset, ...)\n271\t             VALUES (?1, ?2, ?3, ...)\",\n272\t            params![file_path, &file_state.plugin, ...],\n273\t        )?;\n274\t    }\n275\t\n276\t    tx.commit()?;\n277\t\n278\t    // Backup the migrated JSON file\n279\t    let backup_path = json_path.with_extension(\"json.migrated\");\n280\t    std::fs::rename(&json_path, &backup_path)?;\n281\t\n282\t    tracing::info!(count = scrape_state.sources.len(), \"Migrated {} source file states\");\n283\t    Ok(())\n284\t}\n285\t```\n286\t\n287\t### User Experience\n288\t\n289\t**Before migration:**\n290\t```\n291\t~/.agentscribe/state/\n292\t├── scrape-state.json   (21,514 source files, 4MB)\n293\t```\n294\t\n295\t**After migration:**\n296\t```\n297\t~/.agentscribe/state/\n298\t├── scrape-state.db           (SQLite database, ~3MB)\n299\t├── scrape-state.db-wal       (WAL file, small)\n300\t├── scrape-state.db-shm       (WAL shared memory)\n301\t└── scrape-state.json.migrated   (backup, 4MB)\n302\t```\n303\t\n304\t**Logs:**\n305\t```\n306\tINFO agentscribe::scraper::state_sqlite: Migrating scrape state from JSON to SQLite\n307\tINFO agentscribe::scraper::state_sqlite: Migrated 21514 source file states from JSON\n308\tINFO agentscribe::scraper::state_sqlite: legacy=\"scrape-state.json\" backup=\"scrape-state.json.migrated\"\n309\t```\n310\t\n311\t**Cleanup:** Users can safely delete `scrape-state.json.migrated` after confirming migration succeeded.\n312\t\n313\t---\n314\t\n315\t## Performance Characteristics\n316\t\n317\t### Read Performance\n318\t\n319\t- **By file path (primary key):** O(log n) B-tree lookup, typically <1ms for 500K keys\n320\t- **By plugin (indexed scan):** O(log n + k) where k = matching rows, typically <10ms\n321\t- **Full table scan:** O(n) via `get_all()`, ~50ms for 500K rows\n322\t\n323\t### Write Performance\n324\t\n325\t- **Single row update:** O(log n) B-tree traversal + WAL write, typically <2ms\n326\t- **Upsert (INSERT ... ON CONFLICT):** Same as update (one index lookup + write)\n327\t- **Batch import:** ~1000 rows/sec during migration (acceptable one-time cost)\n328\t\n329\t### Concurrency\n330\t\n331\t- **Readers:** Unlimited concurrent readers (WAL mode)\n332\t- **Writers:** Serialized via SQLite's write lock (acceptable for our workload—infrequent writes, no write contention)\n333\t- **Busy timeout:** Configurable (default 30 seconds) — if database is locked, wait up to timeout before returning error\n334\t\n335\t### Disk Usage\n336\t\n337\t**Per-row overhead:** ~100-200 bytes (depending on string lengths)\n338\t\n339\tAt 500K tracked source files:\n340\t- Database size: ~50-100 MB (depends on average file path length)\n341\t- WAL file: ~1-5 MB (checkpointed periodically)\n342\t- Total: <150 MB for 500K keys\n343\t\n344\tCompared to the original 4MB JSON file for 21K files, SQLite scales linearly and predictably.\n345\t\n346\t---\n347\t\n348\t## Configuration and Tuning\n349\t\n350\t### SQLite Pragmas (Current Configuration)\n351\t\n352\t```rust\n353\t// WAL mode for better concurrency\n354\tPRAGMA journal_mode = WAL;\n355\t\n356\t// Relaxed durability (still crash-safe, but faster writes)\n357\tPRAGMA synchronous = NORMAL;\n358\t\n359\t// 2MB cache (small but sufficient for our workload)\n360\tPRAGMA cache_size = -2000;\n361\t\n362\t// Busy timeout (wait up to 30 seconds if database is locked)\n363\tconn.busy_timeout(Duration::from_secs(30));\n364\t```\n365\t\n366\t**Rationale:**\n367\t\n368\t- **WAL mode:** Allows readers to proceed without blocking writers. Critical for daemon + CLI concurrent access.\n369\t- **NORMAL synchronous:** Still crash-safe (fsync on WAL commit), but faster than FULL (fsync on every page write). Acceptable tradeoff for non-critical metadata.\n370\t- **Small cache:** Our workload is infrequent lookups by primary key. Larger cache wouldn't help much.\n371\t- **Busy timeout:** Prevents \"database is locked\" errors during brief concurrent access.\n372\t\n373\t### Future Optimizations\n374\t\n375\tIf performance becomes an issue at scale:\n376\t\n377\t1. **Increase cache size:** `PRAGMA cache_size = -10000` (10MB) for large corpora\n378\t2. **Add more indexes:** If querying by `last_modified` or `last_scraped` becomes common\n379\t3. **Separate table for session IDs:** One row per session for O(1) session addition/removal\n380\t4. **PRAGMA mmap_size = 30000000000:** Memory-map the database for faster reads on large databases\n381\t5. **VACUUM:** Reclaim space after bulk deletes (e.g., `agentscribe gc`)\n382\t\n383\t---\n384\t\n385\t## Operational Considerations\n386\t\n387\t### Backup\n388\t\n389\tSQLite databases are single files (plus WAL). Backups are trivial:\n390\t\n391\t```bash\n392\t# Hot backup (no need to stop writes)\n393\tcp ~/.agentscribe/state/scrape-state.db ~/backup/scrape-state.db\n394\t\n395\t# Or using SQLite CLI\n396\tsqlite3 ~/.agentscribe/state/scrape-state.db \".backup ~/backup/scrape-state.db\"\n397\t```\n398\t\n399\t### Corruption Recovery\n400\t\n401\tIf database becomes corrupted (extremely rare with WAL mode):\n402\t\n403\t```bash\n404\t# Dump to SQL and reimport\n405\tsqlite3 ~/.agentscribe/state/scrape-state.db \".dump\" > dump.sql\n406\tsqlite3 ~/.agentscribe/state/scrape-state-new.db < dump.sql\n407\tmv scrape-state-new.db scrape-state.db\n408\t```\n409\t\n410\t### Monitoring\n411\t\n412\tBasic health checks via CLI:\n413\t\n414\t```bash\n415\t# Check database integrity\n416\tsqlite3 ~/.agentscribe/state/scrape-state.db \"PRAGMA integrity_check;\"\n417\t\n418\t# Check database size\n419\tls -lh ~/.agentscribe/state/scrape-state.db\n420\t\n421\t# Check row count\n422\tsqlite3 ~/.agentscribe/state/scrape-state.db \"SELECT COUNT(*) FROM file_state;\"\n423\t```\n424\t\n425\t### Compaction\n426\t\n427\tIf many rows are deleted (e.g., `agentscribe gc` removes old sessions):\n428\t\n429\t```sql\n430\t-- Manual VACUUM to reclaim space\n431\tVACUUM;\n432\t\n433\t-- Or enable auto-vacuum (at the cost of some performance)\n434\tPRAGMA auto_vacuum = FULL;\n435\t```\n436\t\n437\t---\n438\t\n439\t## Alternatives Considered and Rejected\n440\t\n441\t### LMDB (Lightning Memory-Mapped Database)\n442\t\n443\t**Rejected because:**\n444\t- Requires C library dependency (not pure Rust)\n445\t- Key-value only (no SQL query layer)\n446\t- Less mature tooling than SQLite\n447\t- lmdb-rs crate has mixed maintenance status\n448\t\n449\t### RocksDB\n450\t\n451\t**Rejected because:**\n452\t- Overkill for our use case (designed for petabyte-scale distributed databases)\n453\t- Requires external C++ library (not zero-dependency)\n454\t- Heavy memory and CPU footprint for small datasets\n455\t- Complex configuration and operational overhead\n456\t\n457\t### JSON with append-only log\n458\t\n459\t**Rejected because:**\n460\t- Still O(n) full rewrites for compaction (growing log must be periodically rewritten)\n461\t- No native querying without full scan\n462\t- Reinventing a database poorly\n463\t\n464\t---\n465\t\n466\t## Conclusion\n467\t\n468\tSQLite (via rusqlite with bundled feature) provides the optimal balance of reliability, crash safety, query expressiveness, and zero-dependency deployment for AgentScribe's scrape-state persistence. The implementation is complete, tested, and running in production. Automatic migration from the legacy JSON file ensures a seamless upgrade path for existing users.\n469\t\n470\t**Next Steps:** None (implementation complete and production-ready). Future work may include:\n471\t- Additional indexes for common query patterns\n472\t- Larger cache sizes for large corpora\n473\t- VACUUM integration with `agentscribe gc`\n474\t- Performance benchmarking at 1M+ keys\n475\t\n476\t---\n477\t\n478\t## References\n479\t\n480\t- **ADR-1:** Crash-safe, self-healing persistence for daemon scrape state (2026-07-20)\n481\t- **ADR-2:** Stop storing full session content a second time (2026-07-27)\n482\t- **SQLite Documentation:** https://www.sqlite.org/docs.html\n483\t- **rusqlite Crate:** https://docs.rs/rusqlite/\n484\t- **sled Crate:** https://docs.rs/sled/\n485\t- **redb Crate:** https://docs.rs/redb/\n486\t\n487\t---\n488\t\n489\t**Document Version:** 1.0  \n490\t**Last Updated:** 2026-08-17  \n491\t**Author:** AgentScribe Development Team  \n492\t**Status:** Final - Implementation Complete\n493\t"}]},"parent_tool_use_id":null,"session_id":"88414687-61b9-4602-ae78-176b8ea50e1f","uuid":"02841d46-cba8-443b-9add-9d134f949028","timestamp":"2026-08-17T05:48:30.140Z","tool_use_result":{"type":"text","file":{"filePath":"/home/coding/AgentScribe/docs/notes/state-backend-evaluation.md","content":"# Embedded Database Evaluation for Scrape-State Persistence\n\n**Evaluation Date:** 2026-08-17  \n**Purpose:** Replace JSON-file-based state persistence with a proper embedded database  \n**Use Case:** Track per-source-file scrape state for incremental scraping across 500K+ source files\n\n---\n\n## Executive Summary\n\nAfter evaluating three embedded database options (sled, redb, and SQLite), **SQLite was chosen** as the backend for scrape-state persistence. SQLite provides the best balance of crash safety, concurrency support, mature tooling, and zero external dependencies for this use case.\n\n**Decision:** SQLite (rusqlite with bundled feature)  \n**Status:** ✅ Implemented and default in AgentScribe  \n**Migration:** Automatic one-time import from legacy JSON state file\n\n---\n\n## Use Case Requirements\n\nThe scrape-state persistence layer must support:\n\n1. **O(1) updates by file path** — Update a single source file's state without rewriting the entire corpus\n2. **500K+ keys** — Scale to hundreds of thousands of tracked source files across multiple agents and projects\n3. **Crash-safe writes** — Process kill, disk full, or power loss must not corrupt the entire state database\n4. **Concurrent access** — Multiple scraper workers or daemon + CLI may access state simultaneously\n5. **Cross-platform** — Must work on Linux, macOS, and potentially Windows\n6. **Zero external dependencies** — No system package dependencies or external services\n7. **Embedded** — Single-process database, no separate server process\n\n---\n\n## Comparison Matrix\n\n| Criterion | sled | redb | SQLite (chosen) |\n|-----------|------|------|------------------|\n| **Type** | Pure Rust KV store | Pure Rust B+Tree KV | Relational (embedded) |\n| **Performance (Read)** | Excellent (memory-mapped) | Excellent (memory-mapped) | Good (B-tree index) |\n| **Performance (Write)** | Good | Good | Excellent (WAL mode) |\n| **Concurrency Model** | Lock-free readers + serialized writer | Lock-free readers + serialized writer | WAL mode (multiple readers, serialized writers) |\n| **Memory Footprint** | Moderate (page cache) | Low (copy-on-write tree) | Configurable (default 2MB cache) |\n| **Compaction Behavior** | Background async compaction | Manual compaction required | Automatic via VACUUM or auto-vacuum |\n| **Crash Safety** | Good (copy-on-write) | Good (atomic commits) | Excellent (WAL + rollback) |\n| **Rust Integration** | Native (pure Rust) | Native (pure Rust) | FFI (rusqlite wrapper) |\n| **Maturity** | Stable, actively maintained | Stable, actively maintained | Battle-tested (30+ years) |\n| **Tooling** | Basic CLI | Basic CLI | Extensive (CLI, GUI, diff tools) |\n| **Cross-Platform** | ✅ Yes | ✅ Yes | ✅ Yes |\n| **Single-File Storage** | ✅ Yes | ✅ Yes | ✅ Yes (+WAL files) |\n| **Dependencies** | None (pure Rust) | None (pure Rust) | None (bundled feature) |\n| **Query Expressiveness** | Key-value only | Key-value only | SQL (rich queries, indexes) |\n| **Schema Versioning** | Manual | Manual | Manual (migratable) |\n| **Community Support** | Growing | Growing | Massive |\n\n---\n\n## Detailed Evaluation\n\n### sled\n\n**Strengths:**\n- Pure Rust, idiomatic API\n- Lock-free concurrency model (excellent for read-heavy workloads)\n- Memory-mapped storage for fast reads\n- Copy-on-write B-tree structure provides good crash safety\n- Actively maintained by the FoundationDB community\n\n**Weaknesses:**\n- Background compaction can be unpredictable at scale\n- Less mature tooling compared to SQLite\n- Key-value only (no secondary indexes without manual implementation)\n- Smaller community, fewer battle-tested patterns\n\n**Verdict:** Excellent choice for pure Rust projects needing maximum performance, but overkill for our use case where query complexity is low and reliability matters more than raw throughput.\n\n### redb\n\n**Strengths:**\n- Pure Rust, modern API\n- Copy-on-write B+tree with efficient storage\n- Very low memory footprint\n- Type-safe key-value API\n- Active development\n\n**Weaknesses:**\n- Manual compaction required (can lead to bloat without maintenance)\n- Less mature than sled and SQLite\n- Key-value only (no SQL query layer)\n- Smaller ecosystem\n- Limited real-world deployment history at scale\n\n**Verdict:** A promising modern KV store, but lacks the battle-tested reliability and tooling ecosystem of SQLite. Manual compaction is a operational concern for long-running daemons.\n\n### SQLite (rusqlite)\n\n**Strengths:**\n- Battle-tested reliability (30+ years of production use)\n- WAL mode provides excellent concurrency (multiple readers, serialized writers)\n- Excellent crash safety (rollback journal, atomic commits)\n- Rich query language (SQL) with indexes for complex queries\n- Extensive tooling (CLI, GUI browsers, diff tools)\n- Zero dependencies with `rusqlite`'s bundled feature\n- Automatic schema versioning support\n- Cross-platform with identical behavior\n\n**Weaknesses:**\n- FFI boundary (small performance overhead)\n- Relational overhead for simple KV operations\n- JSON serialization required for complex types (session_ids array)\n- Requires manual pragma tuning for optimal performance\n\n**Verdict:** **Chosen** for our use case. The reliability and tooling ecosystem outweigh the small performance overhead. SQL provides future flexibility for queries like \"all files for plugin X\" without implementing secondary indexes manually.\n\n---\n\n## Why SQLite Was Chosen\n\n### Primary Reason: Crash Safety and Reliability\n\nPer ADR-1, AgentScribe experienced a catastrophic failure where a crashed daemon process left a 4MB JSON state file truncated, blocking scraping for 26 days. SQLite's WAL mode and atomic commits guarantee this cannot happen:\n\n- **Atomic commits:** Either all changes write or none do (no torn writes)\n- **WAL journal:** Write-ahead logging allows rollback of incomplete transactions\n- **Rollback on crash:** If a process crashes mid-transaction, SQLite rolls back the transaction on next open\n- **Hot backups:** Can backup the database without stopping writes\n\n### Secondary Reasons\n\n1. **Tooling Ecosystem:** When debugging state issues, `sqlite3` CLI provides immediate visibility:\n   ```bash\n   sqlite3 ~/.agentscribe/state/scrape-state.db\n   SELECT * FROM file_state WHERE plugin = 'claude-code' LIMIT 10;\n   ```\n\n2. **Query Flexibility:** Future requirements may need complex queries:\n   ```sql\n   -- Find all files that haven't been scraped in 7 days\n   SELECT file_path FROM file_state \n   WHERE datetime(last_scraped) < datetime('now', '-7 days');\n   \n   -- Count files per plugin\n   SELECT plugin, COUNT(*) FROM file_state GROUP BY plugin;\n   ```\n\n3. **Zero Dependencies:** `rusqlite` with the `bundled` feature compiles SQLite into the binary, eliminating the need for `libsqlite3-dev` system packages. This matches AgentScribe's \"no external dependencies\" principle.\n\n4. **Proven at Scale:** SQLite powers Chrome, Firefox, Android, iOS, and countless embedded systems. It handles databases in the hundreds of GB range. At 500K keys (~50MB), it's not even breaking a sweat.\n\n5. **Automatic Migration:** The legacy JSON file migration is simple and proven—load JSON, transactionally insert rows, backup JSON file.\n\n---\n\n## Schema Design\n\n### Table: `file_state`\n\n```sql\nCREATE TABLE file_state (\n    file_path TEXT PRIMARY KEY,\n    plugin TEXT NOT NULL,\n    last_byte_offset INTEGER NOT NULL DEFAULT 0,\n    last_modified TEXT NOT NULL,\n    last_scraped TEXT NOT NULL,\n    session_ids TEXT NOT NULL DEFAULT '[]',\n    last_delimiter_offset INTEGER\n);\n```\n\n### Index\n\n```sql\nCREATE INDEX idx_plugin ON file_state(plugin);\n```\n\nSupports efficient queries like:\n- `SELECT file_path FROM file_state WHERE plugin = 'claude-code'`\n- `files_for_plugin()` implementation in `StateStore` trait\n\n### Schema Version Tracking\n\n```sql\nCREATE TABLE schema_version (version INTEGER PRIMARY KEY);\nINSERT INTO schema_version (version) VALUES (1);\n```\n\nEnables future schema migrations (e.g., adding checksum fields, new columns).\n\n---\n\n## Key-Value Mapping\n\n### Key Format\n\n**Primary key:** `file_path` (full absolute path to source file)\n\nExamples:\n- `/home/user/.claude/projects/-home-coding-myproject/abc123.jsonl`\n- `/home/user/projects/myapp/.aider.chat.history.md`\n\nThis provides O(1) lookups by file path, the primary access pattern for scrape state.\n\n### Value Structure\n\nEach row contains:\n\n| Column | Type | Description |\n|--------|------|-------------|\n| `file_path` | TEXT | Absolute path to source file (primary key) |\n| `plugin` | TEXT | Plugin name (e.g., \"claude-code\", \"aider\") |\n| `last_byte_offset` | INTEGER | Last byte position read (for JSONL position-based resuming) |\n| `last_modified` | TEXT | File modification time (ISO 8601, RFC 3339) |\n| `last_scraped` | TEXT | Last scrape timestamp (ISO 8601, RFC 3339) |\n| `session_ids` | TEXT | JSON array of session IDs extracted from this file |\n| `last_delimiter_offset` | INTEGER | Last delimiter position for markdown-based session detection |\n\n**Why JSON for `session_ids`:** SQLite doesn't have a native array type. Storing as JSON array (`[\"session-1\", \"session-2\"]`) is simple and works well for our use case (small arrays, rarely queried). If this becomes a bottleneck, a separate `file_sessions` table with one row per session ID can be added.\n\n---\n\n## Migration Strategy\n\n### Design Principles\n\n1. **Automatic:** No user intervention required\n2. **One-time:** Migration happens on first load, then never again\n3. **Safe:** Legacy JSON file is backed up before migration\n4. **Atomic:** Migration runs in a single transaction—all-or-nothing\n5. **Detectable:** User can see migration happened in logs and by backup file extension\n\n### Implementation\n\n**Trigger:** First launch after code update, when:\n- `scrape-state.db` does not exist\n- `scrape-state.json` exists (legacy file)\n\n**Steps:**\n\n1. **Detect migration needed:** On `SqliteStateManager::new()`, check if legacy JSON exists\n2. **Create schema:** Initialize `file_state` table and indexes\n3. **Load JSON:** Read `scrape-state.json` and parse as `ScrapeState`\n4. **Begin transaction:** Start SQLite transaction for atomic import\n5. **Import rows:** For each `(file_path, file_state)` in JSON:\n   - Serialize `session_ids` array to JSON string\n   - Format timestamps as RFC 3339\n   - Execute `INSERT INTO file_state ... VALUES (...)`\n6. **Commit transaction:** Atomic commit of all rows\n7. **Backup JSON:** Rename `scrape-state.json` → `scrape-state.json.migrated`\n8. **Log success:** Info-level log with source and backup paths\n\n**Rollback:** If any step fails (JSON parse error, SQLite constraint violation), the transaction is rolled back and the JSON file remains untouched. Next launch retries migration.\n\n### Code Implementation\n\nSee `src/scraper/state_sqlite.rs::migrate_from_json()`:\n\n```rust\nfn migrate_from_json(&self, conn: &mut Connection, json_path: &Path) -> Result<()> {\n    tracing::info!(path = %json_path.display(), \"Migrating scrape state from JSON to SQLite\");\n\n    let json_content = std::fs::read_to_string(json_path)?;\n    let scrape_state: ScrapeState = serde_json::from_str(&json_content)?;\n\n    let tx = conn.transaction()?;\n\n    for (file_path, file_state) in &scrape_state.sources {\n        let session_ids_json = serde_json::to_string(&file_state.session_ids)?;\n        let last_modified = file_state.last_modified.to_rfc3339();\n        let last_scraped = file_state.last_scraped.to_rfc3339();\n\n        tx.execute(\n            \"INSERT INTO file_state (file_path, plugin, last_byte_offset, ...)\n             VALUES (?1, ?2, ?3, ...)\",\n            params![file_path, &file_state.plugin, ...],\n        )?;\n    }\n\n    tx.commit()?;\n\n    // Backup the migrated JSON file\n    let backup_path = json_path.with_extension(\"json.migrated\");\n    std::fs::rename(&json_path, &backup_path)?;\n\n    tracing::info!(count = scrape_state.sources.len(), \"Migrated {} source file states\");\n    Ok(())\n}\n```\n\n### User Experience\n\n**Before migration:**\n```\n~/.agentscribe/state/\n├── scrape-state.json   (21,514 source files, 4MB)\n```\n\n**After migration:**\n```\n~/.agentscribe/state/\n├── scrape-state.db           (SQLite database, ~3MB)\n├── scrape-state.db-wal       (WAL file, small)\n├── scrape-state.db-shm       (WAL shared memory)\n└── scrape-state.json.migrated   (backup, 4MB)\n```\n\n**Logs:**\n```\nINFO agentscribe::scraper::state_sqlite: Migrating scrape state from JSON to SQLite\nINFO agentscribe::scraper::state_sqlite: Migrated 21514 source file states from JSON\nINFO agentscribe::scraper::state_sqlite: legacy=\"scrape-state.json\" backup=\"scrape-state.json.migrated\"\n```\n\n**Cleanup:** Users can safely delete `scrape-state.json.migrated` after confirming migration succeeded.\n\n---\n\n## Performance Characteristics\n\n### Read Performance\n\n- **By file path (primary key):** O(log n) B-tree lookup, typically <1ms for 500K keys\n- **By plugin (indexed scan):** O(log n + k) where k = matching rows, typically <10ms\n- **Full table scan:** O(n) via `get_all()`, ~50ms for 500K rows\n\n### Write Performance\n\n- **Single row update:** O(log n) B-tree traversal + WAL write, typically <2ms\n- **Upsert (INSERT ... ON CONFLICT):** Same as update (one index lookup + write)\n- **Batch import:** ~1000 rows/sec during migration (acceptable one-time cost)\n\n### Concurrency\n\n- **Readers:** Unlimited concurrent readers (WAL mode)\n- **Writers:** Serialized via SQLite's write lock (acceptable for our workload—infrequent writes, no write contention)\n- **Busy timeout:** Configurable (default 30 seconds) — if database is locked, wait up to timeout before returning error\n\n### Disk Usage\n\n**Per-row overhead:** ~100-200 bytes (depending on string lengths)\n\nAt 500K tracked source files:\n- Database size: ~50-100 MB (depends on average file path length)\n- WAL file: ~1-5 MB (checkpointed periodically)\n- Total: <150 MB for 500K keys\n\nCompared to the original 4MB JSON file for 21K files, SQLite scales linearly and predictably.\n\n---\n\n## Configuration and Tuning\n\n### SQLite Pragmas (Current Configuration)\n\n```rust\n// WAL mode for better concurrency\nPRAGMA journal_mode = WAL;\n\n// Relaxed durability (still crash-safe, but faster writes)\nPRAGMA synchronous = NORMAL;\n\n// 2MB cache (small but sufficient for our workload)\nPRAGMA cache_size = -2000;\n\n// Busy timeout (wait up to 30 seconds if database is locked)\nconn.busy_timeout(Duration::from_secs(30));\n```\n\n**Rationale:**\n\n- **WAL mode:** Allows readers to proceed without blocking writers. Critical for daemon + CLI concurrent access.\n- **NORMAL synchronous:** Still crash-safe (fsync on WAL commit), but faster than FULL (fsync on every page write). Acceptable tradeoff for non-critical metadata.\n- **Small cache:** Our workload is infrequent lookups by primary key. Larger cache wouldn't help much.\n- **Busy timeout:** Prevents \"database is locked\" errors during brief concurrent access.\n\n### Future Optimizations\n\nIf performance becomes an issue at scale:\n\n1. **Increase cache size:** `PRAGMA cache_size = -10000` (10MB) for large corpora\n2. **Add more indexes:** If querying by `last_modified` or `last_scraped` becomes common\n3. **Separate table for session IDs:** One row per session for O(1) session addition/removal\n4. **PRAGMA mmap_size = 30000000000:** Memory-map the database for faster reads on large databases\n5. **VACUUM:** Reclaim space after bulk deletes (e.g., `agentscribe gc`)\n\n---\n\n## Operational Considerations\n\n### Backup\n\nSQLite databases are single files (plus WAL). Backups are trivial:\n\n```bash\n# Hot backup (no need to stop writes)\ncp ~/.agentscribe/state/scrape-state.db ~/backup/scrape-state.db\n\n# Or using SQLite CLI\nsqlite3 ~/.agentscribe/state/scrape-state.db \".backup ~/backup/scrape-state.db\"\n```\n\n### Corruption Recovery\n\nIf database becomes corrupted (extremely rare with WAL mode):\n\n```bash\n# Dump to SQL and reimport\nsqlite3 ~/.agentscribe/state/scrape-state.db \".dump\" > dump.sql\nsqlite3 ~/.agentscribe/state/scrape-state-new.db < dump.sql\nmv scrape-state-new.db scrape-state.db\n```\n\n### Monitoring\n\nBasic health checks via CLI:\n\n```bash\n# Check database integrity\nsqlite3 ~/.agentscribe/state/scrape-state.db \"PRAGMA integrity_check;\"\n\n# Check database size\nls -lh ~/.agentscribe/state/scrape-state.db\n\n# Check row count\nsqlite3 ~/.agentscribe/state/scrape-state.db \"SELECT COUNT(*) FROM file_state;\"\n```\n\n### Compaction\n\nIf many rows are deleted (e.g., `agentscribe gc` removes old sessions):\n\n```sql\n-- Manual VACUUM to reclaim space\nVACUUM;\n\n-- Or enable auto-vacuum (at the cost of some performance)\nPRAGMA auto_vacuum = FULL;\n```\n\n---\n\n## Alternatives Considered and Rejected\n\n### LMDB (Lightning Memory-Mapped Database)\n\n**Rejected because:**\n- Requires C library dependency (not pure Rust)\n- Key-value only (no SQL query layer)\n- Less mature tooling than SQLite\n- lmdb-rs crate has mixed maintenance status\n\n### RocksDB\n\n**Rejected because:**\n- Overkill for our use case (designed for petabyte-scale distributed databases)\n- Requires external C++ library (not zero-dependency)\n- Heavy memory and CPU footprint for small datasets\n- Complex configuration and operational overhead\n\n### JSON with append-only log\n\n**Rejected because:**\n- Still O(n) full rewrites for compaction (growing log must be periodically rewritten)\n- No native querying without full scan\n- Reinventing a database poorly\n\n---\n\n## Conclusion\n\nSQLite (via rusqlite with bundled feature) provides the optimal balance of reliability, crash safety, query expressiveness, and zero-dependency deployment for AgentScribe's scrape-state persistence. The implementation is complete, tested, and running in production.
